Chapter: Climate Change 
1.Knowledge Card 
Titel: How this climate change differs from those before 
“Current climate change differs from previous climate changes due to a variety of 
factors that did not exist before. While historic climate changes were mainly natural 
factors such as volcanic activity, solar variability or orbital cycles, this climate change is 
predominantly caused by human activity. Due to the rise in greenhouse gas emissions 
by anthropogenic activity a cooling was reversed and turned into a warming. Not just 
that, but the rate of change is also different. Unlike previous climate changes, this one is 
more rapid and stronger all around the globe. Additionally, the current rate of 
temperature increase exceeds most of known natural climate fluctuations. These 
characteristics indicate that humans are to blame. ” 
Entity List 
Atmospheric window - human influence - cooling – rate of change - climate change - 
past climate variability - climate change now – greenhouse gas emissions 
Relationship triplets 
climate change → differs from → past climate variability 
past climate variability → driven by → natural drivers 
climate change now → driven by → humans 
atmospheric window → window for → longwave radiation 
rate of change → quicker → stronger climate change 
cooling → reversed → warming 
human influence → higher greenhouse gas emissions → rate of change higher 
2.Knowledge Card 
Titel: Evidence for climate change  
“The trends of changes that are occurring in the climate system are recorded using a variety of 
independent variables. The surface and global average temperature are rising because of the 
steady warming of the Earth system. However, the concentration of water vapor in the 
atmosphere as well as an enhanced global atmospheric circulation rate also tends to be rising. 
This has a direct bearing on the rising concentration of precipitation. The rise in sea levels and 

--- PAGE 2 ---
the reduction in the concentration of arctic sea ice is measured through other vital indices. The 
rising surface temperature also contributes toward the reduction of the concentration of snow. 
Additionally, alterations are also noted in the biosphere. It can be noted that the augmentation of 
CO2 concentration tends to induce global greening.” 
Entity List 
Surface temperature – atmospheric circulation - air temperature – water vapour – sea 
level – arctic ice – snow cover – global greening – precipitation – global temperature -
increasing trend – decreasing trend 
Relationship triplets

Snow cover → exhibits → decreasing trend 
Global greening → exhibits → increasing trend 
Precipitation → exhibits → increasing trend 
global temperature → exhibits → increasing trend 
3.Knowledge Card 
Titel: Observed factors for climate change 
“All the following factors are not enough to explain the recent temperature increase but 
one. Orbital change is slow and makes very little changes to the temperature. Solar 
activity as well. Volcanic activity has some spikes but on average remains similar and 
does not increase. Aerosol density even has gone down. Natural greenhouse gas 
emissions barely increased. Combined these factors do not explain the measured 
temperature. Therefore, the conclusion is that humans are the driving factor for this 
climate change. Radiative forcing measures the net change in the energy balance of 
earths system. These years its positive, that means that earth takes in more energy, than 
energy leaves. This leads to warming. ” 
Entity List 
Orbital change – volcanic activity – aerosol density – natural greenhouse gases – human 
activity – recent temperature increase – radiative forcing – solar activity - earths energy 
balance -global warming 
Relationship triplets 
Orbital change → insufficient to explain → recent temperature increase

Earths energy balance → positive → recent temperature increase

4.Knowledge Card 
Titel: Climate resilient development 
“Climate resilient development requires more than short term planning, due to soft 
locks on human activity. Some just don’t want to lower their carbon footprint. Therefore, 
short term planning is just not enough. Long term planning is needed. That includes 
stronger government policies on industry. For example, hydrogen fuel for planes is a 
desperately needed alternative that would lower emissions strongly. Not just but CO2 
removal processes can and should be build or else the climate change risks increase. 
Electrifying public transport would also reduce emissions a lot. Those would be 
essential to reduce the strength of the climate change risks. ” 
Entity List

CO2 removal process – lower emissions – public transport 
Relationship triplets

5.Knowledge Card 
Titel: Heat and Precipitation 

--- PAGE 4 ---
 
“Increasing global warming and climate change lead to increased heat-wave incidence and 
intensity. But this does not necessarily mean that rainfall will also rise. This, of course, can be 
attributed to the fact that hotter air can hold more water vapor. This can lead to increased rainfall 
in some regions but can also induce drought conditions in other regions. Heat-related extremes 
and rainfall extremes can also interact with each other. This can happen if there is an urban heat 
island effect in some regions. This can lead to increased heat stress but can also induce drought 
conditions because of increased rainfall.” 
Entity List 
Global temperature – heat waves – precipitation – atmospheric circulation – moisture 
transport – drought – soil moisture – hydrological cycle – ecosystem stress 
Relationship triplets 
Global temperature → increases → heat waves 
Climate change → affects → atmospheric circulation 
Atmospheric circulation → modifies → precipitation  
Moisture transport → influences → precipitation 
Precipitation → impacts → soil moisture 
Drought → results from → low precipitation 
Heat waves → exacerbate → ecosystem stress 
Hydrological cycle → integrates → heat and precipitation patterns 
Structured Summary 
The reason why the current climate change is different from past natural changes is 
described in this chapter. The past changes in climate were mainly due to natural 
causes. However, the current change in the climate is mainly due to human activities 
that increase the greenhouse gases. The current change in the climate is occurring at a 
rate that is stronger compared to the past natural changes. The rate of change is so fast 
that it does not give the ecosystems enough time to adapt. In the context of adaptations 
in the current change in the climate, this chapter establishes the reason why the 
pressure for biological adaptations is never-before experienced. 
Chapter: (Historic) Pollution in Strato/Troposphere 
1.Knowledge Card 
Titel: Ozone hole 
“The ozone hole refers to a seasonal thinning of stratospheric ozone concentration 
primarily over the Antarctic region. It is caused by humans emitting ozone depleting 

--- PAGE 5 ---
substances such as CFCs and halons, which catalyze ozone destruction in the 
stratosphere during the polar vortex in the Antarctic spring. O3 normally takes in most of 
the ultraviolet light. Once depleted this UV light increasingly reaches earth surface. 
There UV light causes damage to human health via cancer as well as to the ecosystem. 
International regulations aim to reduce ODS emissions which led to a gradual recovery 
layer. While vanishing, the ozone holes are a prime example of anthropogenic influence 
of atmospheric changes. ” 
Entity List 
Ozone hole - polar vortex - ecosystems - human health- ozone depleting substances - 
chlorofluorocarbons (CFCs) – ecosystems – O3 – halons -ozone depletion – ultraviolet 
(UV) radiation 
Relationship triplets 
Ozone- depleting substances → deplete →  O3 
CFCs → type of → ozone depleting substances 
Halons → type of → ozone depleting substances 
Ozone depletion → happens in →  polar vortex 
Ozone depletion → creates → ozone hole 
ozone hole → increases →  ultraviolet (UV) radiation 
UV radiation → impacts → human health 
UV radiation → impacts → ecosystems 
2.Knowledge Card 
Titel: Tropospheric Ozone 
“Ozone can be a pollutant in cities. It forms in cities and not in forests for a reason. It 
needs NOx and high temperatures to photolyze in and form ozone. Both are increasingly 
found in cities. The concrete raises the temperature, and the anthropogenic activity 
increases the NOx concentration via car emissions. Those emissions already impact 
human health. Ozone does this as well. It damages leaves of plants and respiratory 
organs from humans. Ozone also increases with altitude. Hydrocarbons such as OH 
bind ozone very efficiently. Therefore, the problem is that a of a smog ridden city with 
inverse weather layers and little precipitation. These problems barely exist in rural 
areas. ” 
Entity List 
Ozone – troposphere – ozone concentration – light – pollutant – hydrocarbons - cities -
anthropogenic emissions – human health – ecosystem – altitude -temperature – NOx

ozone concentration → rises with → temperature 
cities → produce → anthropogenic emissions 
emissions → impact → human health 
ozone → impacts → ecosystem 
hydrocarbons → bind → ozone 
3.Knowledge Card 
Titel: Oxygen in the earths system 
“The oxidation state of the Earth’s surface and atmosphere has changed quite extensively in the 
geological timescale. The planktons and other prokaryotes in the early Earth contributed to the 
evolution of free oxygen in the atmosphere through photosynthesis reactions. The increase in 
the concentration of free oxygen in the Earth’s atmosphere triggered a few chemical reactions on 
the Earth’s crust and surface. The increased concentration of oxygen in the Earth’s atmosphere 
also accelerated the weathering reactions on the Earth’s surface by facilitating the breakdown of 
rocks and minerals in the presence of water vapor and oxygen gas in the Earth’s atmosphere. 
Water vapor and oxygen gas contributed significantly to the determination of the oxidation state 
on the Earth’s surface and in its oceans, resulting in the current oxidation state that supports life 
on Earth.” 
Entity List 
Oxygen - oxidation level – earth’s crust – historic earth – plankton– atmospheric oxygen -
free oxygen – withering -water vapour 
Relationship triplets 
Earths formation → heat → water vapour 
Water vapour → increases → oxidation level 
Oxidation level → higher than → historic earth 
Oxygen → bound in → stone 
Withering → leads to → rising oxidation levels 
Stone → insufficient explanation for → oxidation levels 

--- PAGE 7 ---
Plankton → photolyzes → oxygen 
Plankton → explains → current oxidation level 
Structured Summary  
The ozone hole is introduced in this chapter as a significant manifestation of 
anthropogenic stratospheric pollution. Ozone-depleting substances, including CFCs 
and halons, catalytically decompose ozone in the Antarctic polar vortex. Consequently, 
ozone depletion is experienced on a seasonal basis. The depletion of ozone in the 
stratosphere is attributed to its absorption of most ultraviolet radiation. As a result, 
increased ultraviolet radiation is allowed to reach the Earth, thus posing significant 
effects on human health and biological life. The ozone hole illustrates, from a climate 
change adaptation perspective, how human-induced changes in the atmosphere 
influence biological processes. 
Quelle: Mitschrift Grundlagen der Ökologie 2 VO  
Chapter: Nischen, Konkurrenz, Koexistenz von Arten, Störungen und 
Wasserkreislauf  
1.Knowledge Card 
Titel: Water flow  
“The water cycle is most important for life on this planet. Water is essential and 
absorbed by plants via rain or soil moisture by their roots and leaves. However, those 
plants and soil release the water back partially via transpiration. The water vapour goes 
back to the atmosphere. The rest of the not taken up water goes back into the ocean. 
There the sun creates clouds via the sun rays making water vaporize. The clouds form 
and partially reach the land surface again. There it rains down again and the water cycle 
is continued. This is called the water cycle and soil-plant-atmosphere continuum. 
Climate changes all of those in amount of water vaporated, raining, rain intensity etc. ” 
Entity List 
Soil – plants -atmosphere – continuum – soil moisture – roots – transpiration – 
evapotranspiration – water cycle – rain – climate change – water amount 
Relationship triplets 
Soil → contains → soil moisture  
Plants → absorb → soil moisture 
Roots → connect → soil and plants 
Plants → release water via → transpiration 

--- PAGE 8 ---
Transpiration → transfers water to → atmosphere 
Soil → loses water via → evapotranspiration 
Evapotranspiration → links → soil and atmosphere 
Atmosphere → transfers water via → rain 
Rain → falls on → soil and plants 
Soil and plants → take up moisture 
Soil-plant-atmosphere continuum → describes –water cycle 
Climate change → changes → water amount 
2.Knowledge Card 
Titel: How plants cope with water scarcity 
“Plants have different strategies to avoid water scarcity such as drought prevention, 
drying delay or drying tolerance. Drought prevention is a method of plants to avoid the 
drought all together by being dormant during this time. This works in Mediterranean 
region where the rainy season comes for certain. Drying delay is a method to prevent 
water scarcity by reducing the water release. This can be done with a thicker cuticula, 
better water storage or larger roots. Drying tolerance is a indicator of how well a plant 
can survive water scarcity and how long they can survive. This means length of survival. ” 
Entity List 
Water scarcity, drought prevention, drying delay, plants, drying tolerance, water release 
– strategies – vegetation period – drought endurance – length of survival 
Relationship triplets 
Plants → avoid → water scarcity 
Plants → have different → strategies 
Plants → avoid water scarcity → drying delay 
Drying delay → reduction of → water release 
Plants → only active during → vegetation period 
Only active during vegetation → is defined as → drought prevention 
Drying tolerance → indicator for → drought endurance 
Drought endurance → means → length of survival 
3.Knowledge Card 
Titel: Leaf interception of precipitation 

--- PAGE 9 ---
“Leaf interception is an important metric for certain forests. In mountainous forests with 
coniferous trees, trees can filter 30-40% more precipitation out of the fog. Due to the 
higher leaf area of coniferous trees, those can filter more precipitation than deciduous 
trees. This strengthens the tree vitality of coniferous trees a lot in mountainous forests. 
Deciduous trees are also less prevalent in mountainous forests and because fog is more 
often in mountainous forests coniferous trees profit a lot more. In deciduous forests 
however, the forests are not as steep. Therefore, via larger throughfall the soil gets more 
water. The water also remains longer here because of this case. ”

4.Knowledge Card 
Titel: Species niches, competition, and coexistence under climate change 
“Climate change changes the conditions of the environments and the resources, temperatures, 
and habitats on which the niches of the species depend. By doing so, when the niches of 
different species change, they could end up competing more actively, as the areas where they 
can thrive overlap strongly. Additionally, due to climate change, there is the potential for the 
geographical areas of different species to overlap in new regions, while in other regions, there 
could be a contraction in the geographical areas because of changes in habits and behaviours, 
as well as changes in the timing of behaviours in search of food and other resources. Climate 
change, in turn, will alter the dynamics between niche differentiation, competition, and 
coexistence, as well as the resulting biodiversity and ecosystem function.” 

--- PAGE 10 ---
 Entity List 
Climate change – ecological niche – species – resource availability -habitat – 
competition – coexistence – range shift – phenology – extinction risk 
Relationship triplets 
Climate change → alter → ecological niche 
Ecological niche → determines → species survival 
Changes in niche → modify → resource availability 
Species → experience → competition 
Competition → affects → coexistence 
Range shift → creates → noval species interactions 
Phenology → influence → competition 
Range contraction → increases → extinction risk 
Flexible niche → enhances → species persistence 
Species → have higher → extinction rate 
5.Knowledge Card 
Titel: Forest use, carbon storage, and ecosystem services 
“Forest usage affects carbon, biodiversity, and ecosystem services. Intensive usage of 
forests leads to lower carbon biomass than what forests can potentially support. In 
Austrian forests, the amount of carbon in forests in circa year 2000 was found to be 
about 30% lower than what it should ideally be. Deadwood is a very important 
component of both carbon pools as well as biodiversity. The rate of decomposition of 
deadwood is very largely influenced by moisture availability. Moist conditions enhance 
the decomposition of wood, whereas dry conditions increase its residence time in 
forests, thereby increasing carbon residence time. The longevity of deadwood in broad-
leaved and coniferous forests is very largely different. Many forest species depend upon 
deadwood, particularly fungi and insects, besides mosses, lichens, birds, and 
amphibians. Assessment of ecosystem service calculations reveals that nature 
conservation-oriented forest management is of highest value to society, whereas 
welfare loss is highest in intensified tree production forests due to loss of cultural 
ecosystem services like biodiversity, protection, and recreation.” 
Entity List 
Forest use – carbon biomass – carbon storage potential – deadwood -decomposition 
rate – moisture conditions – broadleaved trees – coniferous trees – biodiversity – 
ecosystem services 

--- PAGE 11 ---
Relationship triplets 
Forest use→ reduces → carbon biomass 
Carbon biomass → differs from → carbon storage potential 
Deadwood → stores → carbon 
moisture conditions → influence → decomposition rate 
decomposition rate → controls → carbon residence time 
broadleaved trees → differ from → coniferous trees 
deadwood → supports → biodiversity 
biodiversity → contributes to → ecosystem services 
nature conservation → maximizes → ecosystem services 
intense forestry → reduces → cultural ecosystem services 
6.Knowledge Card 
Titel: ecological disturbance 
“Ecological disturbance is an event in time that changes the availability of resources 
and the biomass of an ecosystem. Ecological disturbances have different levels of 
intensity and frequencies, the higher the intensity of the disturbance, the lower the 
frequency, and the lower the intensity of the disturbance, the higher the frequency. 
Larger-scale disturbance can include fire and wind, and the disturbance can span the 
whole forest, while the other disturbance is ecological, taking place on a different, 
smaller scale. For example, a falling tree branch will form an ecological disturbance in a 
very small ecosystem, like the ground in the forest, due to changes in space and 
resources.” 
Entity List 
ecological disturbance – resource availability – biomass – temporally defined event – 
high disturbance intensity – low disturbance intensity – occurrence frequency – fire – 
wind – falling branch 
Relationship triplets 
ecological disturbance → change of → resource availability 
ecological disturbance → reduce → biomass 
ecological disturbance → is → temporally defined event 
high disturbance intensity → reduces → occurrence frequency 
low disturbance intensity → increases → occurrence frequency 

--- PAGE 12 ---
fire → is → ecological disturbance 
wind → is → ecological disturbance 
falling branch → is → ecological disturbance 
Structured Summary 
The current chapter describes how climate change impacts basic ecologic processes 
underlying species niche construction, coexistence, and competitive interactions. 
Temperature and precipitation-driven changes occur within the soil-plant atmosphere 
continuum and influence water availability, drought stress conditions, and various 
strategies of plants like drought avoidance, escape, or tolerance. Changes in 
precipitation regimes affect leaf fogging, soil moisture levels, and forest type 
distribution, especially impairing coniferous forests under fog reduction. Niche 
construction under climate conditions accelerates competing interactions and 
extinction risk factors due to disturbances such as fire, winds, or small events that 
reorganize and redistribute resources and bandwood mass. 
Chapter: Insects and their adaptations 
1.Knowledge Card 
Titel: adaptations of insects to low temperatures 
“Cold temperatures are a significant threat to insects due to their ability to decrease 
metabolic processes and induce ice crystal formation, which may injure insect cells by 
freezing. However, to cope with low temperatures, there are behavioural and 
physiological adaptations in insects to reduce damage from freezing temperatures. One 
such method involves seeking shelter in soil, bark, and leaf litter, which shields the 
insects from temperatures in their surroundings. Additionally, some insects undergo 
aggregation, a behavioural technique that helps to maintain their body temperatures by 
acting as a buffer zone to protect them from cold temperatures. Such adaptations lower 
the effects of freezing temperatures and ensure survival in cold temperatures 
experienced in the winter season.” 
Entity List 
Ice crystals – cell structures – insects – protected habitat – low temperatures -metabolic 
functions – protected habitat – freezing damage – aggregation – thermal buffering 
Relationship triplets 
Ice crystals → damage → cell structures 
Low temperatures → disrupt → metabolic functions 
Insects → avoid  → ice crystal formation 
Insects → seek → protected habitats 

--- PAGE 13 ---
Protected habitat → reduces → temperature extremes 
Insects → form → aggregation 
Aggregation → provides → thermal buffering 
Thermal buffering → improves → cold survival 
2.Knowledge Card 
Titel: adaptations of insects to high temperatures 
“Insects must deal with the problem of high temperatures, which impact cellular 
activities. High temperatures can cause the denaturing of proteins, damage to cellular 
membranes, and interference with metabolic activities, resulting in reduced 
functionality and survivability. High temperatures can, in turn, cause oxygen limitation, 
which is a constraint on metabolism. High-temperature environments are created by 
bright sunlight and prolonged exposure to the sun. Insects have several behavioural and 
morphological adaptations to deal with the problem of high temperatures. Insects have 
silver scales on their bodies, reflecting sunlight and thereby decreasing the absorption 
of heat. Other insects migrate to different habitat areas during bright sun time, when 
temperatures in the surrounding, such as the ground, are lower and quite stable.” 
Entity List 
High temperature – proteins – oxygen – cell membrane – metabolic functions - sunlight – 
insects – silver scales -sun hours - ground 
Relationship triplets 
High temperature → denatures → proteins 
high temperature → creates a lack of → oxygen 
high temperature → damages → cell membrane 
high temperature → inhibits → some metabolic functions 
sunlight → causes → high temperatures 
insects → reflect the sunlight via → silver scales 
insects → avoid → sun hours 
insects → dig into → ground 
3.Knowledge Card 
Titel: influence of climate change on ectothermic animals 
“Ectothermic species are those which rely upon environmental temperatures to govern 
their body as well as physiological processes. Climate change affects the temperature 
regimes, which spills over into greater heat stress, optimal temperatures, as well as time 

--- PAGE 14 ---
of activity. An increase in temperature may speed up the rate of metabolism but may 
also surpass physiological thresholds of tolerable temperatures, making it way below 
optimal levels of performance, survival, as well as reproduction. Precipitation, as well as 
extreme events, increase the habitat range. This, in effect, causes range shifts, time 
shifts, as well as a dependence upon habitat the physiological responses of ectothermic 
species.” 
Entity List 
Ectothermic animals – climate change – temperature – metabolic rate – thermal 
tolerance – activity period – habitat suitability – range shifts - microhabitats 
Relationship triplets 
Ectothermic animals → depend on → environmental temperature 
Climate change → alters → temperature  
Temperature → controls → metabolic rate 
High temperatures → exceed → thermal tolerance 
Climate change → reduces → habitat suitability 
Ectothermic animals → use → microhabitat 
Climate change → drives → range shifts 
Structured Summary 
This chapter explains the behavioral, physiological, and morphological adaptations that 
enable insects and other ectothermic animals to cope with temperature stress and 
climate change. Low temperatures threaten insects by disrupting metabolism and 
through ice-crystal damage, countered by shelter-seeking and aggregation behaviors 
that provide thermal buffering. High temperatures impair proteins, membranes, and 
oxygen supply, but insects mitigate heat stress through reflective body structures, 
temporal avoidance of sun exposure, and utilizing cooler microhabitats such as soil. In 
contrast, climate change is increasing both heat and cold extremes, is shifting 
temperature regimes, and is altering activity periods, habitat suitability, and species 
ranges. These adaptive responses are crucial for insect survival in the face of increasing 
climatic variability. 
Quelle: Mitschrift Forstpathologie VO/UE  
Chapter: Climate Change winners 
1.Knowledge Card 
Titel: Diplodia sapinea 

--- PAGE 15 ---
“Diplodia sapinea has been recognized as a fungal pathogen of conifers, especially 
Pinus species. A warmer climate and more stressed conditions are ideal for infections 
due to the plant's reduced resistance. The fungal pathogen leads to shoot blights, tips 
dieback, and cankers on infected plants. A warmer environment advances the growth 
rate of the fungi, the production of spores, and the spread of diseases. The success of 
the Diplodia sapinea amid climate change proves that fungi can effectively benefit from 
host stress and extended growth seasons. The control of this particular pathogen largely 
relies on monitoring stands that are prone to infection.” 
Entity List 
Diplodia sapinea – conifers -Pinus nigra – drought stress – shoot blight -tip dieback – 
cankers -spore production – climate change 
Relationship triplets 
Diplodia sapinea → infects → Pinus spp. 
drought stress → increases → host susceptibility 
climate change → accelerates → fungal growth 
fungal growth → increases → spore production 
spore production → enhances → disease spread 
infection → causes → shoot blight 
infection → causes → tip dieback 
infection → causes → cankers 
climate change → favors → Diplodia sapinea 
2.Knowledge Card 
Titel: Cryptostroma corticale – Rußrindenkrankheit 
“Cryptostroma corticale is a fungal pathogen that causes sooty bark disease, mainly in 
Acer pseudoplatanus (sycamore maple). The fungus infects stressed or drought-
weakened trees, and it colonizes the inner bark and phloem while producing black, 
powdery spores on the surface of the bark. The symptoms include dieback, cracking of 
the bark, discoloration, and heavy deposits of spores, which may result in reduced tree 
growth and may kill the tree eventually. Human beings who are exposed to these spores 
may have irritation that results in respiratory or allergic reactions. Warm and dry 
seasons, which most of the time are attributed to climate change, favor the proliferation 
of the pathogen and enhance the severity of the disease. It includes maintaining tree 
health by removing the diseased bark or trees and surveillance of susceptible stands. 
Cryptostroma corticale is a good example of how fungi pathogens take advantage of 
environmental stress as climate change winners within the temperate forests.” 

--- PAGE 16 ---
Entity List 
Crypotstroma corticale – acer pseudoplatanus – inner bark – phloem – spores – dieback 
– dieback -bark cracking – drought stress – growth reduction – climate change 
Relationship triplets

infection → colonizes → inner bark 
infection → colonizes → phloem

spores → deposit on → bark surface 
infection → causes → dieback 
infection → causes → bark cracking

infection → reduces → tree growth 
climate change → favors → Cryptostroma corticale 
3.Knowledge Card  
Titel: Kretzschmaria deusta – Brandkrustenpilz 
“Kretzschmaria deusta, or Brittle Cinder or Brandkrustenpilz, is a wood decay fungus 
that infects decidual trees like Fagus sylvatica and other hardwood. Kretzschmaria 
deusta infect roots or lower trunk, causing white rot, and growing due to elevated 
demands from environmental stress, such as drought, from climate change. Climate 
change as such increases tree susceptibility due to these elevated demands, making 
Kretzschmaria deusta infested trees even more susceptible. Kretzschmaria deusta is 
therefore an excellent example of a wood decay fungus that survives and prospers 
because of climate change, as it infects trees that are not as resilient due to high 
demands from drought. He is an endophyte, as he responds to tree susceptibility due to 
tree weakness, making him a clear climate change winner.” 
Entity List 
Kretzschmaria deusta - wood decay fungus – Fagus sylvatica – roots -lower stem -white 
rot - drought stress – spore production – climate change 
Relationship triplets 
Kretschmaria deusta → is already in → beech 
Kretschmaria deusta → waits for → weak beech 
Drought stress → causes → weak beech 

--- PAGE 17 ---

Kretschmaria deusta → infects → roots 
Kretschmaria deusta → infects → lower stems

4.Knowledge Card 
Titel: Armillaria spp. 
“Armillaria spp., also known as Hallimasch, are wood-decaying fungi that function as 
facultative parasites on several varieties of temperate forest tree species, including 
Fagus sylvatica, Picea abies, Pinus spp. They infect weakened tree hosts that encounter 
Armillaria roots, producing white rot. These fungi function as weak parasites since 
Armillaria mainly infect weakened tree hosts rather than healthy ones. Climate change 
acts as a factor that enhances Armillaria success due to increasing drought, storms, 
and stress factors that weaken tree hosts. Furthermore, increased warming enhances 
Armillaria growth, rhizomorph production, and spore production. Armillaria spp. are 
recognized as ultimate winners due to their ability to thrive within changing climatic 
conditions. These organisms contribute to forest decay processes.” 
Entity List 
Armillaria spp. – Fagus sylvatica – Picea abies -Pinus spp. – facultative parasite - 
weakened trees – white rot – rhizomorphs -climate change – drought - storm damage 
Relationship triplets 
Armillaria spp. → is a → facultative parasite 
Armillaria spp. → infects → weakened trees 
Infection → causes → white rot 
Armillaria spp. → colonizes → roots and deadwood 
Weakened trees → result from → drought 
Weakened trees → result from → storm damage 

--- PAGE 18 ---
Climate change → increases → host stress 
Warmer temperatures → favour → fungal growth 
Armillaria spp. → spread via → rhizomorphs 
Climate change → enhances → Armillaria spp. Success 
Structured Summary 
This chapter focuses on fungal pathogens, which are clear climate change winners in 
forest ecosystems. Species include Diplodia sapinea, Cryptostroma corticale, 
Kretzschmaria deusta, and the Armillaria spp. that benefit from temperature increase, 
drought stress, and extremes that weaken tree hosts. Climate change depresses tree 
resistance and extends growing seasons, enhancing fungal growth, spore production, 
and disease spread. Many of these fungi are opportunists or weak parasites that infect 
stressed hosts rather than healthy trees. Their success demonstrates how climate-
driven disturbances shift forest dynamics toward higher pathogen pressure, increased 
tree mortality, and altered ecosystem functioning, underlining the importance of 
adaptation and monitoring in forest management.

Chapter: Ecology 
1.Knowledge Card 
Titel: Biotic factors and their changing influence due to climate change 
“Biotic factors are living organisms in an ecosystem. Those include plants, insects, fungi 
and microorganisms. Those factors influence each other through countless interactions 
such as parasitism, mutualism, predation etc... Climate change alters temperature and 
moisture levels, which changes the strength of those interactions. For example, bark 
beetles (parasites for spruce in this case) thrive under the drought stress of their hosts = 
host stress. Same applies to some fungi. Therefore, they change the population 
dynamics. As a result, balance is shifted. Existing niches may break, others created. 
Pathogens and parasites often capitalize on the stress of the not yet adapted hosts. ” 
Entity List 
Biotic factors – climate change – species interactions – competition – parasite – 
parasitism – host stress – population dynamics - host 

--- PAGE 19 ---
Relationship triplets 
Biotic factors → include → living organisms 
biotic factors → regulate → species interactions 
climate change → alters → environmental interactions 
competition → shapes → population dynamics 
parasitism → increase under → host stress 
parasite → may thrive under → climate change 
parasite → becomes greater danger for → host 
climate change → increases → host stress 
biotic factors → influence → ecosystem functioning 
2.Knowledge Card 
Titel: abiotic factors and their changing influence due to climate change 
“Abiotic factors are the non-living components of ecosystems such as temperature, 
precipitation, water availability etc… They define the environmental limits in which 
organisms can survive and function. Climate change alters all those and especially 
intensifies or amplifies them. For example, the droughts become longer and 
precipitation becomes stronger in intensity. These changes affect metabolic rates, water 
balance, and habitat suitability. As abiotic conditions shift, species are forced to adapt, 
move, or decline. An abiotic shift therefore forces adaptation from the ecosystem fit for 
those new environmental limits. In doing so, they shape the ecosystem, its health and 
the diversity of species. ” 
Entity List 
abiotic factors – climate change – temperature – sun light – water availability – soil 
conditions – climate change – environmental limits – metabolic rate - habitat 
Relationship triplets 
abiotic factors → include → temperature 
abiotic factors → include → precipitation 
precipitation → affects → soil conditions 
abiotic factors → define → environmental limits 
climate change → alters → abiotic factors 
temperature → affects → metabolic rate 
precipitation → controls → water availability 

--- PAGE 20 ---
abiotic factors → determine → habitat 
climate change → drives → ecosystem change 
3.Knowledge Card 
Titel: Climate change and range shifts of species 
“Temperature and humidity represent the most critical abiotic elements defining the 
distribution of various plant and animal species. Climate change affects these 
elements, meaning that their distribution zones will continue changing. Projections 
estimate that the range limits of distribution zones, particularly in the north, will shift 
considerably towards the north during centuries to come. Adaptability to these changes 
will depend largely on dispersal ability. Proof of range limits shifting through fossil pollen 
evidence indicates changes during past climate periods. Nevertheless, contemporary 
climate change proceeds considerably faster, leaving many distribution zones 
vulnerable to a loss of access to appropriate climatic conditions.” 
Entity List 
Climate change – temperature – moisture – species distribution – northward shift – range 
boundary – dispersal ability – range shift – future distribution - dispersal ability – 
environmental limits 
Relationship triplets 
climate change → alters → temperature 
climate change → alters → moisture 
temperature → determines → species distribution 
moisture → determines → species distribution 
climate change → causes → northward shift of range boundary 
range boundary → defines → species distribution 
dispersal ability → limits → range shift 
fossil pollen → documents → past range shifts 
future distribution → depends on → dispersal ability 
low dispersal ability →  may cause death by → changing environmental limits 
4.Knowledge Card 
Titel: Limitations on the spread of tree species 
“After the end of the last ice age, the large ice glaciers retreated, which opened new 
habitats for tree growth. These gradually spread northwards, but the rate at which they 
migrated was restricted by the rate at which the seeds could move. Fossil pollen studies 

--- PAGE 21 ---
are able to accurately trace the post-glacial migration range of the trees. The case of the 
American Beech tree, Fagus grandifolia, is able to show the delay response by tree 
plants when responding to global warming. Their migration range was considerably 
slower than the rate at which the earth warmed up.” 
Entity List 
Postglacial warming – glacier retreat – northward expansion – tree species – seed 
dispersal – dispersal speed – migration rate – American beech – Fagus grandifolia – 
migration lag  
Relationship triplets 
Postglacial warming → caused → glacier retreat 
Glacier retreat → allowed → northward expansion of tree species 
Tree species → migrate via → seed dispersal 
Dispercal speed → limits → migration rate 
Fossil pollen → records → tree migration 
American beech → is → Fagus grandifolia 
Fagus grandifolia → showed → migration lag 
Migration lag → occurs when → climate warming faster than dispersal 
Structured Summary 
In this chapter, the impacts of climate change on ecological systems and how it affects 
abiotic and biotic factors are fully explained. Abiotic factors such as temperature and 
precipitation shape the environment and influence metabolic rates and habitat 
requirements that are being altered by climate change. Additionally, climate change 
impacts biotic interactions between species, including competition and host-parasite 
associations, where climate change often favors the parasite as a result of host stress. 
Range extensions, normally towards the north and up-hill, are also caused by climate 
change; however, many species are constrained by their migratory capacity, resulting in 
a lag in their range and consequently high extinction risks. 
Chapter: The influence of humans on the Earth's biogeochemical 
cycles 
1.Knowledge Card 
Titel: Effects of elevated CO2 on forest growth – the FACTS-I experiment 
“The study of the Forest-Atmosphere Carbon Transfer and Storage (FACTS-I) at Duke 
University examines the implications of the artificially elevated concentration of CO₂ on 
a forest of incense pine trees (_Pinus taeda) since the year 1995. The experimental 

--- PAGE 22 ---
treatment involves the supply of enriched CO₂ to circular plot areas using a ring of 
towers. After the tenth year of the study, the effect of the treatment is evident and 
beneficial; tree growth is increased. Nonetheless, this is well below what could be 
anticipated in the earlier greenhouse studies because of limiting factors like available 
nitrogen. Half the plots were fertilized since the year 2005.” 
Entity List 
FACTS-I experiment – CO2 – forest growth – nutrient availability – nitrate - ammonium – 
fertilization – carbon storage – human activity – climate change – scientists

Tree growth → is limited by → nutrient availability 
Scientists → fertilize → ammonium and nitrate 
Fertilization → leads to → faster tree growth 
FACTS-I experiment → shows → CO2 effect smaller than predicted 
2.Knowledge Card 
Titel: CO2 increase and global temperature correlation 
“Caused by the burning of fossil fuels and deforestation, the tropospheric concentration 
of CO₂ has constantly increased since the Industrial Revolution from about 274 ppm to 
more than 400 ppm today. This continuous rise has been reliably documented by the 
measuring station on Mauna Loa/Hawaii since 1958. In parallel, despite higher 
fluctuations, a clear trend toward a rise in global average temperature can be observed. 
Climate models predict a doubling of the CO₂ concentration and a temperature increase 
of 3°C by the end of the 21st century in the case of unchanged emissions. However, 
analyses of prehistoric data from ice cores show that historical temperature increases 
sometimes preceded the rise in CO₂, and thus this question is debated scientifically 
regarding causality and correlation in the currently observed warming.” 
Entity List 
Fossil fuel burning - CO2 concentration – Deforestation – Mauna Loa Observatory - CO2 
increase – global temperature – climate models – warming -ice core data – ancient air 
bubbles -CO2 levels - sequence 
Relationship triplets 
Fossil fuel burning → causes → rise in CO2 concentration 

--- PAGE 23 ---
Deforestation → causes → rise in CO2 concentration 
CO2 concentration → is measured at → Mauna Loa Obersatory 
CO2 increase → correlates with → rise in global temperature  
Climate models → predict → doubling of CO2 and 3°C warming by 2100 
Ice core data → contain → ancient air bubbles 
Ancient air bubbles → reveal → historic CO2 levels and temperatures 
Historical data → show that → temperature rise can precede CO2 increase 
This sequence → fuels debate on → correlation vs. causation 
3.Knowledge Card 
Titel: Impact of elevated CO₂ on C3 vs. C4 plants 
“One of the expected outcomes related to high CO₂ levels is the increased productivity 
of plants, especially those from the C3 group like wheat and soybeans. As the 
availability of CO₂ is more restrictive to the photosynthetic process of plants belonging 
to the C3 group compared to plants from the C4 species like maize, the increased 
availability of CO₂ could alter the balance. Plants belonging to the C3 species will thrive 
well under the influence of high CO₂ levels and could displace the other species. Still, 
the fourth type benefits more under high temperatures because of more effective 
photosynthesis.” 
Entity List 
Elevated CO2 levels - CO2 fertilization – C4 plants – C3 plants – dynamic – crop 
competition – high temperature – productivity – species composition - CO2 
Relationship triplets 
Elevated CO2  levels → causes a → CO2 fertilization effect 
CO2 fertilization effect → benefits more → C3 plants 
C4 plants → are less limited by → CO2 availability 
C4 plants → have an advantage at → higher temperatures 
This dynamic → influences → crop competition 
This dynamic → influences → productivity 
Future species composition → depend on interaction with → CO2 
4.Knowledge Card 
Titel: The enhanced greenhouse effect and polar amplification 

--- PAGE 24 ---
“Long-lived gases, such as CO₂, CH₄, N₂O, as well as artificial gases such as 
hydrofluorocarbons (HFCs) and sulfur hexafluoride (SF₆), act as greenhouses since they 
absorb infrared radiation given off by the Earth's surface but do not absorb the visible 
part of the sun's radiation. This natural Greenhouse Effect maintains the Earth's average 
temperatures at +15°C, thus making the conditions adequate for living. The increased 
Greenhouse Effect has increased warming. This warming is particularly large in the 
northern-most areas of the globe. In these areas, the thawing of ice and snow leads to 
increased absorption of radiation, thus enhancing warming, in addition to the thawing of 
permafrost. The abrupt decrease in summer ice in the Arctic poses an immediate risk to 
the living environments of polar bears, seals, and seabirds.” 
Entity List 
Greenhouse gases – infrared radiation – concentration – anthropogenic effect – warming 
– northern latitudes – snow and ice – surface albedo – feedback loop – arctic sea ice – 
animal habitats – boreal forests and tundra 
Relationship triplets 
Greenhouse gases → absorb and re-radiate → infrared radiation 
Increased concentrations → enhance → anthropogenic effect 
Increased concentrations → lead to → warming 
Warming → strongest in → northern latitudes 
Warming → increasingly melts → snow and ice 
Snow and ice melting → reduces → surface albedo 
Lower surface albedo → causes a positive → feedback loop 
This feedback → accelerates melting of → arctic sea ice 
Loss of arctic sea ice → endangers → animal habitats 
Boreal forests and tundra → are → most affected 
5.Knowledge Card 
Titel: Scientific debate on causality and its ecological irrelevance 
“The great majority of scientists are persuaded that today’s global warming is a 
consequence of the growth in greenhouse gases released by human activities. 
Nevertheless, in the prehistoric series of ice core samples, it has been found that in the 
past, the growth in temperatures was preceded by a rise in CO₂ concentration in the 
atmosphere, and, hence, in some instances, temperatures may rise because of other 
causes such as orbital variations and then CO₂ emissions. This scientific controversy is 
extremely interesting and debated among academics. Nevertheless, as far as ecology is 
concerned, it’s irrelevant because living organisms are faced with both phenomena.” 

--- PAGE 25 ---
Entity List 
Paleoclimate date – ice core - CO2 lag – pattern – causality debate – scientific 
consensus – human activity – current warming -ecological perspective – organisms – 
greenhouse gas concentrations – temperatures – exposure – ecosystem change – 
ecological adaptation 
Relationship triplets 
Paleoclimate data → measured in old → ice core 
Paleoclimate data → shows instance of → CO2 lag after temperature rise 
This pattern → triggered → causality debate 
Scientific consensus → attributes → current warming to human activity 
Ecological perspective → considers → causality debate less relevant 
Organisms → experience → increasing greenhouse gas concentrations 
Organisms → experience → rising temperatures 
Combined exposure → drives → ecosystem change 
Ecosystem change → forces → ecological adaptation 
Structured Summary 
This subchapter provides a description of how human activities significantly alter the 
Earth's carbon cycle and climate system. Combustion of fossil fuel and deforestation 
increase atmospheric CO₂ concentrations, which are driving global warming via an 
enhanced greenhouse effect and polar amplification. Increased CO₂ can have a 
fertilizing effect on forest growth and C3 plant productivity, as indicated by experiments 
like FACTS-I, but this effect is limited by nutrient availability. Climate change alters the 
species composition, productivity, and functioning of ecosystems, while positive 
feedbacks involving processes like ice-albedo interactions accelerate heating. Although 
paleoclimate records of CO₂ and temperature are complex, organisms are subjected to 
a synchronous increase in temperature and greenhouse gas concentrations in a way 
that forces ecological adaptation and ecosystem change.

Kapitel: Einleitung, Gefahren bei der Schadholzaufarbeitung, 
Vorgehensweise bei der Schadholzaufarbeitung 
1.Knowledge Card 
Titel: Einleitung 
„Aufgrund extremer Wetterphänomene wie Stürmen, Starkregenereignissen und daraus 
resultierenden Überflutungen wird die heimische Forstwirtschaft in den letzten Jahren 
mit großen Problemen und Herausforderungen konfrontiert. Zusätzlich stellt die 
Aufarbeitung von Schadholz eine der gefährlichsten Tätigkeiten überhaupt dar. Daher 
besteht die Notwendigkeit, nur gut ausgebildetes Personal einzusetzen, 
Sicherheitsbestimmungen einzuhalten und entsprechend fachgerechte 
Arbeitstechniken zur Aufarbeitung von Windwurfflächen anzuwenden. “  
Entity List  
Wetterphänomene – Stürme – Starkregenereignisse – Windwurfflächen -

Starkregenereignisse → führen zu → Überflutungen 
Schadholzaufarbeitung → gilt als → gefährlichste Tätigkeit 
Personal → benötigt → gute Ausbildung 
Arbeitstechniken →dienen → Windwurfaufarbeitung  
2.Knowledge Card 
Titel: Gefahren bei der Schadholzaufarbeitung 
„Die möglichen Gefahren bei der Windwurfaufarbeitung sind umfassend. Folgende 
Gefahren sind die am häufigsten auftretenden und stellen ein besonderes Risiko für 
arbeitendes Personal dar Abrollende oder Umkippende Wurzelballenteller, welche 
Personen erschlagen, begraben oder erdrücken können; Spannungen im Stamm, die zu 
Ausschlagen des Stammes oder gar aufreißen führen können; Angeschobene und 
angelehnte Bäume, welche ebenfalls eine Gefahr des Erschlagens darstellen; Geknickte 
und abgebrochene Bäume, können in unerwartete Richtungen schnellen; 
Durcheinanderliegende Stämme und Bäume, welche zu einer Unübersichtlichkeit der 
Situation führen und ein falsches herangehen provozieren können; Ein generelles 
nichtwahrnehmen und Unterschätzen von akuten Gefahrensituationen. “  

--- PAGE 27 ---
Entity List

3.Knowledge Card 
Titel: Vorgehensweise bei der Schadholzaufarbeitung 
„Um Unfälle vorzubeugen, schnell, sicher und effizient zu arbeiten, erfordert die 
Aufarbeitung von Windwurfflächen eine universell an jede Schadholzsituation 
anwendbare Vorgehensweise. Diese besteht aus fünf Punkten in Reihenfolge, welche 
notwendig sind, um das Motto „Sicherheit geht vor (unüberlegter) Schnelligkeit“ zu 
gewährleisten. 1.) Sollte man sich den bestmöglichen Überblick und Schadensausmaß 
einer Waldfläche verschaffen. So ist hier festzustellen, ob es sich um einzelne 
Windwürfe oder Flächenwürfe handelt. 2.) Nun stellt sich die Frage, mit welchem 
Arbeitsverfahren (z.B. Seilgerät, Harvester, Bagger, Seilwinde etc.) man die Fläche 
aufarbeitet und ob die Situation Maschineneinsatz erfordert. Hierbei gilt, dass bei 
größeren Windwurfflächen der Einsatz von Harvester und Forwarder nahezu 
unumgänglich ist. 3.) Kontrolle und Überprüfung der Persönlichen Schutzausrüstung 
(PSA), Arbeitsmittel und Erste-Hilfe-Material. Ebenso die Mitnahme eines Handys mit 
Empfang zur eventuellen Abgabe eines Notrufs und ortskenntliche Lagebeschreibungen 
sind zu gewährleisten. 4.) Ausschließlicher Einsatz von geschultem und möglichst 
erfahrenem Personal. 5.) Zuletzt gilt Waldarbeit ist keine Alleinarbeit! Besonders bei der 
Aufarbeitung von Schadholzflächen ist ein Team aus mehreren Waldarbeitern 
erforderlich. Ebenso kann überlegt werden, ob eine Überbetriebliche Zusammenarbeit 
zweckdienlich ist. “ 
Entity List  
Vorgehensweise – Überblick – Flächenwürfe – Kontrolle – Waldarbeit – Notruf – 
Teamarbeit 
Relationship Triplets  
Vorgehensweise → dient zur → Unfallvorbeugung 

--- PAGE 28 ---
Überblick → bestimmt → Schadensausmaß 
Flächenwürfe → erfordern oft → Harvester und Forwarder 
Kontrolle → hier bezogen auf → PSA, Erste-Hilfe und Handy 
Waldarbeit → ist keine → Alleinarbeit 
Teamarbeit → gewährleistet → Sicherheit vor Schnelligkeit 
Notruf → benötigt → Ortskenntliche Lagebeschreibung 
Structured Summary 
In den vergangenen Jahren stellen extreme Wetterereignisse wie Stürme und Starkregen 
die heimische Forstwirtschaft vor massive Herausforderungen. Besonders die 
Aufarbeitung von Schadholz auf Windwurfflächen zählt zu den gefährlichsten Tätigkeiten 
im Wald. Die Risiken sind vielfältig Instabile Wurzelballenteller können umkippen, unter 
Spannung stehende Stämme unvorhersehbar ausschlagen und verkeilte Bäume 
herabstürzen. Oft führt zudem die Unübersichtlichkeit der Flächen dazu, dass Gefahren 
unterschätzt werden. Um unter diesen Bedingungen sicher und effizient zu arbeiten, ist 
eine strukturierte, fünfstufige Vorgehensweise unerlässlich, die dem Grundsatz 
„Sicherheit vor Schnelligkeit“ folgt: 
1. Lagebeurteilung Zunächst muss das gesamte Schadensausmaß analysiert 
werden, um zwischen Einzelwürfen und großflächigen Schäden zu 
unterscheiden. 
2. Verfahrenswahl Es wird entschieden, welche Technik zum Einsatz kommt. Bei 
großen Flächen ist der Einsatz von schwerem Gerät wie Harvestern und 
Forwardern meist unumgänglich, um das Risiko für den Menschen zu 
minimieren. 
3. Ausrüstungscheck Die persönliche Schutzausrüstung (PSA), Arbeitsmittel und 
Erste-Hilfe-Sets müssen lückenlos überprüft werden. Zudem ist die 
Notfallkommunikation durch Handys und präzise Ortsbeschreibungen 
sicherzustellen. 
4. Qualifikation Es sollte ausschließlich geschultes und erfahrenes Fachpersonal 
eingesetzt werden, dass die fachgerechten Arbeitstechniken beherrscht. 
5. Teamarbeit Da Waldarbeit niemals in Alleinarbeit erfolgen darf, ist ein 
eingespieltes Team oder sogar eine überbetriebliche Kooperation zwingend 
erforderlich. 
Durch diese konsequente Planung und den Einsatz professioneller Strukturen lassen 
sich die enormen Risiken der Schadholzaufarbeitung beherrschen und Unfälle wirksam 
vermeiden. 

--- PAGE 29 ---
Kapitel: Trennschnitttechniken bei Wurzelballen 1, 
Trennschnitttechniken bei Wurzelballen 2, Vorhänger, Extremer
